========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.01, F50.02\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, yeah, thanks for seeing me. Um, so I guess I'm here because... well, it's kind of a long story, but it basically boils down to how I've been eating lately. I'm a firefighter, and a little while ago I decided I really wanted to get on the lieutenant track. It's super competitive, and you need to be just physically completely dialed in, setting an example for the rest of the house. So I started eating cleaner. You know, skipping the heavy, greasy comfort food the guys always cook at the station, focusing on being lean and efficient. \n\nBut what started as just, you know, trying to be healthier and sharper for the promotion has sort of... turned into me cutting my meals down drastically. Like, way down. It's gotten to the point where my whole day is just black coffee in the morning, a protein shake later on, and then just a really small dinner. And the thing that's really getting to me—and I know this is why I probably need to be talking to someone—is that I'm doing this even on shift days. \n\nShift days are incredibly demanding, you know? You're in full turnout gear, hauling heavy equipment, running drills, or actually fighting a fire and sweating out half your body weight. I know for an absolute fact that I burned way, way more energy than what I'm taking in with just coffee and a shake. I know the math doesn't add up. But I just keep doing it anyway. It's like I get stuck on this routine and I just can't bring myself to eat a normal meal with the crew anymore, even when I know I've worked myself into the ground that day.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you're intentionally restricting your food intake because you're worried about gaining weight or changing your body shape?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... honestly, yes. That's exactly what it is, and it's so frustrating to even admit it out loud because it sounds so superficial, but it's really not about vanity. It's... well, in the firehouse, there's this intense culture, right? Everyone is always watching, always sizing each other up. And I've gotten so hyper-focused on this lieutenant promotion that my body shape feels like my resume. \n\nSo I weigh myself constantly now. And if I step on that scale and it's up even a single pound—like, logically, I know it could just be from drinking extra water after a training evolution, or maybe from one of those big station meals if I actually gave in and ate a little bit of what the guys made—but if I see that number go up even a tiny fraction, I get this... this really intense, tight panic in my chest. It's this overwhelming feeling that I've completely blown it. All that discipline, just gone in an instant. \n\nAnd my mind immediately starts racing, thinking that everyone is going to notice. I get terrified that the crew is going to look at me and think I'm getting soft, or that I'm just totally out of control of my own body and my own life. And in this job, if people think you're soft, they don't trust you to haul a 200-pound victim out of a structure fire. You have to be a rock. So that panic just sits with me all day. I'll spend the entire shift obsessing over that one pound, just fixating on how I need to cut back even more to fix it, because the fear just completely overrides the fact that I'm probably just retaining water. It just convinces me that I'm losing my edge, and I just can't let that happen.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you've lost control and eaten large amounts of food in a short period of time, or have you ever made yourself sick or done anything extreme like excessive exercise or using laxatives to try to control your weight?\n\nPatient: No, no, I haven't done anything like that. I mean, I know what you're talking about—you hear stories about people who just snap and eat everything in the kitchen, or, you know, make themselves throw up or take pills. But that's not me. I don't lose control like that. Actually, it's almost the exact opposite. It's all about control for me. \n\nLike, if I were to just sit down and eat a massive amount of food, that would mean I completely broke my discipline, and I just don't let that happen. Even when the guys at the station are ordering pizzas at 2 AM after a rough call, and the smell is just filling the whole bay, and I'm starving—I mean, my stomach is literally cramping because I haven't eaten anything solid since that protein shake twelve hours ago—I still don't cave. I just drink more water or maybe have another black coffee, even though it wrecks my sleep. \n\nAnd as for making myself sick or taking laxatives... no, absolutely not. That just seems so counterproductive to what I'm trying to achieve. I'm trying to be this elite, disciplined firefighter, a machine, basically. Doing stuff like that feels chaotic, you know? It feels messy. And I don't do excessive exercise outside of what's required for the job, either. I mean, the job itself is excessive exercise. We have mandatory PT, we're running stairs with 70 pounds of gear, we're doing hose deployment drills. By the time my shift is over, I'm so physically drained that the idea of going for a run or hitting the gym for another two hours is just impossible. I just rely on the restriction. Just keeping the intake as low as possible. So, yeah, no bingeing, no throwing up, none of that stuff. Just... just sticking to the plan, no matter how hungry I get.\n\nDoctor: When you think about why you continue to restrict your food so severely, is it mainly about maintaining a certain body weight or shape, or is it more about sticking to the routine and feeling like you’re in control of yourself and your habits?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, it's... it's a bit of both, I guess, but it really comes back to the shape and the weight. I mean, the routine is important, sure, but the routine is just the tool to get the result. And the result is what I'm completely fixated on. \n\nLike, every single morning before I head out to the station for my shift, I have this whole ritual. I stand in front of the bathroom mirror—the lighting in there is really bright, so it shows absolutely everything—and I just scrutinize myself. I'm looking specifically at my waist and my face. I need to see that sharpness, you know? I need to see that the jawline is defined, that there's no softness around the middle where the duty belt sits. Because if I look in that mirror and I feel like I look a little softer, or a little rounded, or just not as lean and cut as I was the day before, it completely derails me. \n\nIt's not just about vanity, like I said before. It's about how I feel when I walk into that firehouse. If I don't see that sharpness in the mirror, it totally messes with my head for the rest of the day. I lose that sense of confidence. I start feeling like I don't look the part of a leader, like I'm not command-ready. And when you're trying to prove you have what it takes to be a lieutenant, you have to project absolute authority and physical readiness every second you're on the floor. If I feel like my body isn't reflecting that elite standard, I feel like an imposter in my own uniform. It just ruins my focus. I'll spend the whole shift pulling at my shirt, wondering if the other guys are noticing that I look a little off, feeling like I have no business telling anyone else what to do if I can't even keep my own body perfectly dialed in. So, yeah, it's definitely about the shape and the weight, because that's what dictates whether I feel like I'm actually good enough to be there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your overall sense of self-worth or how you judge yourself is strongly tied to your body shape or weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, absolutely. I mean, it's exactly like what I was just saying a second ago about the mirror. That whole routine where I check my waist and my face before work to see if they look sharp enough? That’s not just about looking good in the uniform. If I don't see that sharpness, it completely messes with how confident and command-ready I feel, but it goes way deeper than just my mood for the day. It literally dictates my entire sense of self-worth. It’s like... if I’m not the leanest, most disciplined guy in the room, then who am I? What value do I even bring? \n\nI’ll be sitting in the rig, right, heading out to a call with the sirens blaring, and instead of just running through the tactical plan in my head, there’s this loud voice in the back of my mind telling me I’m a failure just because I thought my jawline looked a little soft at 5 AM. It sounds insane when I say it out loud, I know it does. But in my head, my body shape is my worth. If I’m cut and lean, I’m a good firefighter, I’m a strong leader, I’m someone who deserves respect and that lieutenant promotion. But if I feel even slightly off, if I feel like I’ve lost even a fraction of that physical edge, I judge myself so harshly. I feel like a total fraud. \n\nIt's like my entire identity is built on this one specific physical standard. I feel like I don't even deserve to wear the badge or tell anyone else what to do if I'm not physically perfect. I'll spend hours just beating myself up over it, telling myself I'm weak or lazy, even though I know how hard I work. So, yeah... I can't really separate who I am as a person from what the mirror shows me or what the scale says. They're just completely tied together at this point, and I don't even know how to untangle them anymore. It's just all one and the same to me.\n\nDoctor: Has your focus on your body shape or weight caused problems in your relationships, work, or other important areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Well, as far as the actual work goes, no, it hasn't caused problems there. I’m still doing my job, and I’m doing it well. I mean, when the tones drop, I’m the first one on the rig, gear on, ready to work. I’m still pulling my weight on the fireground, maybe even more so because I feel like I have to prove myself constantly. So tactically, functionally, I'm fine. \n\nBut... yeah, I guess it is causing problems in other ways, specifically with the guys. You have to understand, in a firehouse, the kitchen is everything. It’s the absolute center of the universe. That’s where you bond, where you debrief after a rough call, where you bust each other's chops and build that trust. Station meals are a massive tradition. But lately, I’ve just... I’ve started ducking out of station meals completely. \n\nLike, if it's our turn to cook and someone is making a huge baked ziti, or if the crew decides to pool money and order a ton of wings or Chinese food, I just can't be around it. I’ll find any excuse to be somewhere else. I’ll tell the captain I need to go do a deep clean of the compartments on the engine, or I’ll go inventory the EMS bags for the third time that shift, or I’ll just hide out in the bunk room and pretend I'm intensely studying for the lieutenant's exam. I just keep to myself when the crew orders food. \n\nAnd the thing is, it’s really starting to isolate me. I’m definitely less part of the team than I used to be, and that... that really gets to me, because the brotherhood is honestly the best part of this job. At first, the guys just thought I was on some hardcore temporary health kick. They’d make the usual jokes, call me a rabbit, offer me a piece of lettuce, whatever. Just standard firehouse ribbing. But now... now it’s just awkward. They don't even bother calling me over the PA when dinner's ready anymore. I’ll be out in the apparatus bay by myself, wiping down tools, and I can hear them in the day room, laughing and telling stories and just being a crew, and I'm entirely on the outside of it. \n\nIt feels like there's this thick glass wall between me and the rest of my shift now. I’m still working right beside them, trusting them with my life in a fire, but I’m not really *with* them anymore. And I hate it. I want to sit at that table and joke around like I used to, but the thought of sitting there, having to either eat what they're eating or have them stare at me while I drink black coffee... the anxiety is just too loud. So I just keep avoiding them, even though I know it's ruining my relationships at the station.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your body is heavier or less toned than it actually is, even when others might tell you otherwise?\n\nPatient: It’s honestly really confusing because, logically, I know the facts, right? Like, I have the physical evidence right in front of me. My gear and my station clothes... they’re hanging so much looser on me now. I wear these standard-issue navy uniform pants around the firehouse, and lately, I’m having to cinch my belt up a couple of extra notches just to keep them from sliding down when I’m walking around the apparatus bay. And my actual turnout gear, the heavy stuff we wear into a fire? I feel like I’m practically swimming in my bunker pants these days. I had to adjust my suspenders all the way down just so the crotch doesn't hang at my knees. So physically, I know things have changed. \n\nAnd on top of that, we actually just had our annual departmental fitness reviews. It’s this whole mandatory physical and agility test you have to do to prove you’re fit for duty. Well, the medical officer actually flagged my chart this time. He pulled me into his office, shut the door, and gave me this whole serious speech because he said my weight was officially too low for my height and my natural build. He was looking right at the numbers on the scale, telling me I need to be careful, asking if I was feeling weak or lightheaded on calls, making this huge deal out of it and acting like I was on the verge of collapsing on the fireground. \n\nBut... and this is where I know I sound completely irrational... even after he said all that, and even with my clothes literally bagging off me, I walked out of that medical review and immediately thought, well, he doesn't know what he's talking about. Because when I look in the mirror, I just don't see a guy whose weight is too low. I still see softness. I still see areas that aren't defined enough, especially around my waist. So even with the department doctor flagging me and telling me I'm underweight, part of me still feels like I should keep trimming down just a little more. \n\nIt's like I can't trust what anyone else is saying, or even what my own clothes are doing, because my brain is just convinced that I'm still heavier than I need to be to get that perfect, command-ready look I was talking about. I'll stand there pulling at my shirt, thinking that if I just lean out a tiny bit more, I'll finally look the way I'm supposed to. I just keep thinking, just a little more, just a little more, and it doesn't matter what the fitness review says, because I just don't see what they see.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience significant distress or anxiety when you are unable to follow your dietary routine or when you are exposed to situations where others are eating foods you consider unhealthy?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I wouldn't really say I get distressed about a routine, because, honestly, I don't really have one of those strict routines to begin with. Like, you know those guys at the station, the total gym rats? They’ll bring in their stack of little meal-prep Tupperware containers with exactly six ounces of boiled chicken breast and half a cup of plain brown rice, and if the tones drop for a call and they end up missing their perfectly scheduled 12:00 PM feeding, they completely lose their minds. They get all cranky and start complaining about losing their gains or whatever. \n\nI’m not like that at all. I don't have this rigid schedule where I have to eat a certain thing at a certain time, so there isn't really a routine for me to mess up or get anxious over. Mostly, I'm just... you know, skipping it, or just grabbing a black coffee, or maybe something really small if I absolutely have to so I don't feel shaky. So if a call goes long—like last week, we had this massive three-alarm commercial building fire that kept us out on the scene for like eight hours straight—I don't panic about missing a meal. If anything, it's just easier. I don't have to think about it.\n\nAnd as for other people eating stuff... nah, that doesn't bother me at all. I mean, I know I just said a minute ago that I avoid the big station dinners, but that's not because seeing the food itself makes me anxious, or because I'm judging the guys for what they're eating. I really don't care if the crew is housing a dozen glazed donuts from the bakery down the street or ordering deep-dish pizza. They can eat whatever they want, they work hard, they burn it off. I don't look at them and feel distressed or think, \"Wow, that's so unhealthy.\" \n\nIf I'm just walking through the day room and Smitty is sitting in the recliner eating a massive meatball sub, I don't feel any anxiety about *him* eating it. I’ll still stop and talk to him for a second about the baseball game last night. The problem for me is just the expectation that I'm supposed to be eating it *with* them. It's the social pressure, the guys offering me a slice and then staring at me when I say no. That’s what I avoid. But just being exposed to other people eating junk food? Or my own schedule getting thrown off by a call? No, I haven't really noticed any anxiety about that kind of stuff. It's really just focused on my own body, not what anyone else is doing.\n\nDoctor: Even though you’ve told me that your uniform and turnout gear are getting loose and the department doctor told you your weight was too low, do you still feel like you need to lose more weight or that you’re not lean enough?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Yeah, I do. It’s... it’s like a constant tape playing in my head, honestly. You'd think hearing the medical officer tell me I'm too light would be this big relief, right? Like, \"Okay, I did it, I can stop now.\" But it doesn't work like that at all. It’s almost the opposite. Because now there's this massive, overwhelming fear of going backwards. \n\nLike, we have this old analog scale in the locker room at the station—you know, the heavy-duty medical kind with the metal sliding weights that clank around when you step on it. I check it pretty much every single shift. And if that scale is up... I mean, even just one single pound... I completely lose it internally. Even if I can logically tell myself, \"Hey, you just drank a ton of extra water because you were doing overhaul on a fire in 90-degree heat,\" or maybe it's just from a big station meal if I actually forced myself to eat a few bites just to get the guys off my back... it doesn't matter. The logic just completely vanishes. \n\nI get this immediate, tight panic in my chest. It’s this horrible physical sensation, like my lungs can't expand, and my brain just starts screaming that I’ve completely blown it. All I can think is that if I gain even an ounce, it's a slippery slope, and suddenly everyone in the department is going to look at me and see me as soft. They're going to think I'm weak, or that I'm just totally out of control of myself and my body. \n\nAnd in my line of work, being out of control is the absolute worst thing you can be. If you're out of control, you're a liability to the guy next to you in a burning building. You have to be disciplined. So, yeah, even with my bunker pants practically falling off me and the doc pulling me into his office to give me warnings, the thought of gaining weight just terrifies me. I just feel like I have to keep pushing, keep leaning out, just to make absolutely sure I never give anyone a reason to look at me and think I've lost my edge.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. No new information was introduced to rule out either diagnosis definitively. The patient maintains a significantly low weight, has an intense fear of gaining weight, and exhibits disturbance in self-perceived weight or shape. However, the absence of a strict dietary routine or anxiety around disruptions to routine, combined with the patient's high physical activity level and occupation, leaves the differential open between typical and atypical anorexia.", "is_final": false}

I don't look at them and feel distressed or think, \"Wow, that's so unhealthy.\" \n\nIf I'm just walking through the day room and Smitty is sitting in the recliner eating a massive meatball sub, I don't feel any anxiety about *him* eating it. I’ll still stop and talk to him for a second about the baseball game last night. The problem for me is just the expectation that I'm supposed to be eating it *with* them. It's the social pressure, the guys offering me a slice and then staring at me when I say no. That’s what I avoid. But just being exposed to other people eating junk food? Or my own schedule getting thrown off by a call? No, I haven't really noticed any anxiety about that kind of stuff. It's really just focused on my own body, not what anyone else is doing.\n\nDoctor: Even though you’ve told me that your uniform and turnout gear are getting loose and the department doctor told you your weight was too low, do you still feel like you need to lose more weight or that you’re not lean enough?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Yeah, I do. It’s... it’s like a constant tape playing in my head, honestly. You'd think hearing the medical officer tell me I'm too light would be this big relief, right? Like, \"Okay, I did it, I can stop now.\" But it doesn't work like that at all. It’s almost the opposite. Because now there's this massive, overwhelming fear of going backwards. \n\nLike, we have this old analog scale in the locker room at the station—you know, the heavy-duty medical kind with the metal sliding weights that clank around when you step on it. I check it pretty much every single shift. And if that scale is up... I mean, even just one single pound... I completely lose it internally. Even if I can logically tell myself, \"Hey, you just drank a ton of extra water because you were doing overhaul on a fire in 90-degree heat,\" or maybe it's just from a big station meal if I actually forced myself to eat a few bites just to get the guys off my back... it doesn't matter. The logic just completely vanishes. \n\nI get this immediate, tight panic in my chest. It’s this horrible physical sensation, like my lungs can't expand, and my brain just starts screaming that I’ve completely blown it. All I can think is that if I gain even an ounce, it's a slippery slope, and suddenly everyone in the department is going to look at me and see me as soft. They're going to think I'm weak, or that I'm just totally out of control of myself and my body. \n\nAnd in my line of work, being out of control is the absolute worst thing you can be. If you're out of control, you're a liability to the guy next to you in a burning building. You have to be disciplined. So, yeah, even with my bunker pants practically falling off me and the doc pulling me into his office to give me warnings, the thought of gaining weight just terrifies me. I just feel like I have to keep pushing, keep leaning out, just to make absolutely sure I never give anyone a reason to look at me and think I've lost my edge.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's... I guess it's hard to separate them, honestly. I wouldn't say it's *exclusively* my weight or my shape, because my job performance means absolutely everything to me. Being a firefighter isn't just what I do, it's who I am, you know? I take a massive amount of pride in being good at my job, knowing my equipment, being someone the guys can rely on when things go sideways. So my self-worth is definitely tied to that. 

But the thing is... they’re kind of tangled up together now. I can't really separate how I feel about my body from how I feel about my job, because one completely dictates the other. 

Like, every single morning before work, before I even put on my station uniform, I have this... I guess you could call it a routine, though I don't really want to do it. I get up, and I go straight to the bathroom mirror. And I'll just stand there under those harsh overhead lights, turning side to side, and I just intensely focus on whether my waist and my face look sharp enough. I'm looking for that specific definition, checking if my jawline is cut, checking if my stomach is completely flat and tight. I'll stand there pulling my shirt tight against my torso, just analyzing every little angle. 

And if they don’t look sharp—if I wake up and I feel like my face looks even a tiny bit puffy, or if my waist feels soft to me—it completely ruins everything. It completely messes with how confident and command-ready I feel that day. 

Because in this line of work, you have to project authority, right? When we roll up on a scene, whether it's a medical call or a structure fire, people are usually having the worst day of their lives. They look at us to take control of the chaos. And if I look in the mirror that morning and feel like I look soft, I just... I don't feel like I belong in that uniform. I feel like an imposter. I'll spend the entire 24-hour shift second-guessing myself, adjusting my posture, worrying that the captain or the public is looking at me and thinking I don't have the discipline to be there. So, yeah, my job and being a good firefighter are a huge part of how I see myself, but if my body doesn't look exactly the way I think it needs to in that mirror, it just totally strips away all that confidence before I even walk through the bay doors.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.01, F50.02\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, yeah, thanks for seeing me. Um, so I guess I'm here because... well, it's kind of a long story, but it basically boils down to how I've been eating lately. I'm a firefighter, and a little while ago I decided I really wanted to get on the lieutenant track. It's super competitive, and you need to be just physically completely dialed in, setting an example for the rest of the house. So I started eating cleaner. You know, skipping the heavy, greasy comfort food the guys always cook at the station, focusing on being lean and efficient. \n\nBut what started as just, you know, trying to be healthier and sharper for the promotion has sort of... turned into me cutting my meals down drastically. Like, way down. It's gotten to the point where my whole day is just black coffee in the morning, a protein shake later on, and then just a really small dinner. And the thing that's really getting to me—and I know this is why I probably need to be talking to someone—is that I'm doing this even on shift days. \n\nShift days are incredibly demanding, you know? You're in full turnout gear, hauling heavy equipment, running drills, or actually fighting a fire and sweating out half your body weight. I know for an absolute fact that I burned way, way more energy than what I'm taking in with just coffee and a shake. I know the math doesn't add up. But I just keep doing it anyway. It's like I get stuck on this routine and I just can't bring myself to eat a normal meal with the crew anymore, even when I know I've worked myself into the ground that day.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you're intentionally restricting your food intake because you're worried about gaining weight or changing your body shape?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... honestly, yes. That's exactly what it is, and it's so frustrating to even admit it out loud because it sounds so superficial, but it's really not about vanity. It's... well, in the firehouse, there's this intense culture, right? Everyone is always watching, always sizing each other up. And I've gotten so hyper-focused on this lieutenant promotion that my body shape feels like my resume. \n\nSo I weigh myself constantly now. And if I step on that scale and it's up even a single pound—like, logically, I know it could just be from drinking extra water after a training evolution, or maybe from one of those big station meals if I actually gave in and ate a little bit of what the guys made—but if I see that number go up even a tiny fraction, I get this... this really intense, tight panic in my chest. It's this overwhelming feeling that I've completely blown it. All that discipline, just gone in an instant. \n\nAnd my mind immediately starts racing, thinking that everyone is going to notice. I get terrified that the crew is going to look at me and think I'm getting soft, or that I'm just totally out of control of my own body and my own life. And in this job, if people think you're soft, they don't trust you to haul a 200-pound victim out of a structure fire. You have to be a rock. So that panic just sits with me all day. I'll spend the entire shift obsessing over that one pound, just fixating on how I need to cut back even more to fix it, because the fear just completely overrides the fact that I'm probably just retaining water. It just convinces me that I'm losing my edge, and I just can't let that happen.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you've lost control and eaten large amounts of food in a short period of time, or have you ever made yourself sick or done anything extreme like excessive exercise or using laxatives to try to control your weight?\n\nPatient: No, no, I haven't done anything like that. I mean, I know what you're talking about—you hear stories about people who just snap and eat everything in the kitchen, or, you know, make themselves throw up or take pills. But that's not me. I don't lose control like that. Actually, it's almost the exact opposite. It's all about control for me. \n\nLike, if I were to just sit down and eat a massive amount of food, that would mean I completely broke my discipline, and I just don't let that happen. Even when the guys at the station are ordering pizzas at 2 AM after a rough call, and the smell is just filling the whole bay, and I'm starving—I mean, my stomach is literally cramping because I haven't eaten anything solid since that protein shake twelve hours ago—I still don't cave. I just drink more water or maybe have another black coffee, even though it wrecks my sleep. \n\nAnd as for making myself sick or taking laxatives... no, absolutely not. That just seems so counterproductive to what I'm trying to achieve. I'm trying to be this elite, disciplined firefighter, a machine, basically. Doing stuff like that feels chaotic, you know? It feels messy. And I don't do excessive exercise outside of what's required for the job, either. I mean, the job itself is excessive exercise. We have mandatory PT, we're running stairs with 70 pounds of gear, we're doing hose deployment drills. By the time my shift is over, I'm so physically drained that the idea of going for a run or hitting the gym for another two hours is just impossible. I just rely on the restriction. Just keeping the intake as low as possible. So, yeah, no bingeing, no throwing up, none of that stuff. Just... just sticking to the plan, no matter how hungry I get.\n\nDoctor: When you think about why you continue to restrict your food so severely, is it mainly about maintaining a certain body weight or shape, or is it more about sticking to the routine and feeling like you’re in control of yourself and your habits?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, it's... it's a bit of both, I guess, but it really comes back to the shape and the weight. I mean, the routine is important, sure, but the routine is just the tool to get the result. And the result is what I'm completely fixated on. \n\nLike, every single morning before I head out to the station for my shift, I have this whole ritual. I stand in front of the bathroom mirror—the lighting in there is really bright, so it shows absolutely everything—and I just scrutinize myself. I'm looking specifically at my waist and my face. I need to see that sharpness, you know? I need to see that the jawline is defined, that there's no softness around the middle where the duty belt sits. Because if I look in that mirror and I feel like I look a little softer, or a little rounded, or just not as lean and cut as I was the day before, it completely derails me. \n\nIt's not just about vanity, like I said before. It's about how I feel when I walk into that firehouse. If I don't see that sharpness in the mirror, it totally messes with my head for the rest of the day. I lose that sense of confidence. I start feeling like I don't look the part of a leader, like I'm not command-ready. And when you're trying to prove you have what it takes to be a lieutenant, you have to project absolute authority and physical readiness every second you're on the floor. If I feel like my body isn't reflecting that elite standard, I feel like an imposter in my own uniform. It just ruins my focus. I'll spend the whole shift pulling at my shirt, wondering if the other guys are noticing that I look a little off, feeling like I have no business telling anyone else what to do if I can't even keep my own body perfectly dialed in. So, yeah, it's definitely about the shape and the weight, because that's what dictates whether I feel like I'm actually good enough to be there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your overall sense of self-worth or how you judge yourself is strongly tied to your body shape or weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, absolutely. I mean, it's exactly like what I was just saying a second ago about the mirror. That whole routine where I check my waist and my face before work to see if they look sharp enough? That’s not just about looking good in the uniform. If I don't see that sharpness, it completely messes with how confident and command-ready I feel, but it goes way deeper than just my mood for the day. It literally dictates my entire sense of self-worth. It’s like... if I’m not the leanest, most disciplined guy in the room, then who am I? What value do I even bring? \n\nI’ll be sitting in the rig, right, heading out to a call with the sirens blaring, and instead of just running through the tactical plan in my head, there’s this loud voice in the back of my mind telling me I’m a failure just because I thought my jawline looked a little soft at 5 AM. It sounds insane when I say it out loud, I know it does. But in my head, my body shape is my worth. If I’m cut and lean, I’m a good firefighter, I’m a strong leader, I’m someone who deserves respect and that lieutenant promotion. But if I feel even slightly off, if I feel like I’ve lost even a fraction of that physical edge, I judge myself so harshly. I feel like a total fraud. \n\nIt's like my entire identity is built on this one specific physical standard. I feel like I don't even deserve to wear the badge or tell anyone else what to do if I'm not physically perfect. I'll spend hours just beating myself up over it, telling myself I'm weak or lazy, even though I know how hard I work. So, yeah... I can't really separate who I am as a person from what the mirror shows me or what the scale says. They're just completely tied together at this point, and I don't even know how to untangle them anymore. It's just all one and the same to me.\n\nDoctor: Has your focus on your body shape or weight caused problems in your relationships, work, or other important areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Well, as far as the actual work goes, no, it hasn't caused problems there. I’m still doing my job, and I’m doing it well. I mean, when the tones drop, I’m the first one on the rig, gear on, ready to work. I’m still pulling my weight on the fireground, maybe even more so because I feel like I have to prove myself constantly. So tactically, functionally, I'm fine. \n\nBut... yeah, I guess it is causing problems in other ways, specifically with the guys. You have to understand, in a firehouse, the kitchen is everything. It’s the absolute center of the universe. That’s where you bond, where you debrief after a rough call, where you bust each other's chops and build that trust. Station meals are a massive tradition. But lately, I’ve just... I’ve started ducking out of station meals completely. \n\nLike, if it's our turn to cook and someone is making a huge baked ziti, or if the crew decides to pool money and order a ton of wings or Chinese food, I just can't be around it. I’ll find any excuse to be somewhere else. I’ll tell the captain I need to go do a deep clean of the compartments on the engine, or I’ll go inventory the EMS bags for the third time that shift, or I’ll just hide out in the bunk room and pretend I'm intensely studying for the lieutenant's exam. I just keep to myself when the crew orders food. \n\nAnd the thing is, it’s really starting to isolate me. I’m definitely less part of the team than I used to be, and that... that really gets to me, because the brotherhood is honestly the best part of this job. At first, the guys just thought I was on some hardcore temporary health kick. They’d make the usual jokes, call me a rabbit, offer me a piece of lettuce, whatever. Just standard firehouse ribbing. But now... now it’s just awkward. They don't even bother calling me over the PA when dinner's ready anymore. I’ll be out in the apparatus bay by myself, wiping down tools, and I can hear them in the day room, laughing and telling stories and just being a crew, and I'm entirely on the outside of it. \n\nIt feels like there's this thick glass wall between me and the rest of my shift now. I’m still working right beside them, trusting them with my life in a fire, but I’m not really *with* them anymore. And I hate it. I want to sit at that table and joke around like I used to, but the thought of sitting there, having to either eat what they're eating or have them stare at me while I drink black coffee... the anxiety is just too loud. So I just keep avoiding them, even though I know it's ruining my relationships at the station.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your body is heavier or less toned than it actually is, even when others might tell you otherwise?\n\nPatient: It’s honestly really confusing because, logically, I know the facts, right? Like, I have the physical evidence right in front of me. My gear and my station clothes... they’re hanging so much looser on me now. I wear these standard-issue navy uniform pants around the firehouse, and lately, I’m having to cinch my belt up a couple of extra notches just to keep them from sliding down when I’m walking around the apparatus bay. And my actual turnout gear, the heavy stuff we wear into a fire? I feel like I’m practically swimming in my bunker pants these days. I had to adjust my suspenders all the way down just so the crotch doesn't hang at my knees. So physically, I know things have changed. \n\nAnd on top of that, we actually just had our annual departmental fitness reviews. It’s this whole mandatory physical and agility test you have to do to prove you’re fit for duty. Well, the medical officer actually flagged my chart this time. He pulled me into his office, shut the door, and gave me this whole serious speech because he said my weight was officially too low for my height and my natural build. He was looking right at the numbers on the scale, telling me I need to be careful, asking if I was feeling weak or lightheaded on calls, making this huge deal out of it and acting like I was on the verge of collapsing on the fireground. \n\nBut... and this is where I know I sound completely irrational... even after he said all that, and even with my clothes literally bagging off me, I walked out of that medical review and immediately thought, well, he doesn't know what he's talking about. Because when I look in the mirror, I just don't see a guy whose weight is too low. I still see softness. I still see areas that aren't defined enough, especially around my waist. So even with the department doctor flagging me and telling me I'm underweight, part of me still feels like I should keep trimming down just a little more. \n\nIt's like I can't trust what anyone else is saying, or even what my own clothes are doing, because my brain is just convinced that I'm still heavier than I need to be to get that perfect, command-ready look I was talking about. I'll stand there pulling at my shirt, thinking that if I just lean out a tiny bit more, I'll finally look the way I'm supposed to. I just keep thinking, just a little more, just a little more, and it doesn't matter what the fitness review says, because I just don't see what they see.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience significant distress or anxiety when you are unable to follow your dietary routine or when you are exposed to situations where others are eating foods you consider unhealthy?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I wouldn't really say I get distressed about a routine, because, honestly, I don't really have one of those strict routines to begin with. Like, you know those guys at the station, the total gym rats? They’ll bring in their stack of little meal-prep Tupperware containers with exactly six ounces of boiled chicken breast and half a cup of plain brown rice, and if the tones drop for a call and they end up missing their perfectly scheduled 12:00 PM feeding, they completely lose their minds. They get all cranky and start complaining about losing their gains or whatever. \n\nI’m not like that at all. I don't have this rigid schedule where I have to eat a certain thing at a certain time, so there isn't really a routine for me to mess up or get anxious over. Mostly, I'm just... you know, skipping it, or just grabbing a black coffee, or maybe something really small if I absolutely have to so I don't feel shaky. So if a call goes long—like last week, we had this massive three-alarm commercial building fire that kept us out on the scene for like eight hours straight—I don't panic about missing a meal. If anything, it's just easier. I don't have to think about it.\n\nAnd as for other people eating stuff... nah, that doesn't bother me at all. I mean, I know I just said a minute ago that I avoid the big station dinners, but that's not because seeing the food itself makes me anxious, or because I'm judging the guys for what they're eating. I really don't care if the crew is housing a dozen glazed donuts from the bakery down the street or ordering deep-dish pizza. They can eat whatever they want, they work hard, they burn it off. I don't look at them and feel distressed or think, \"Wow, that's so unhealthy.\" \n\nIf I'm just walking through the day room and Smitty is sitting in the recliner eating a massive meatball sub, I don't feel any anxiety about *him* eating it. I’ll still stop and talk to him for a second about the baseball game last night. The problem for me is just the expectation that I'm supposed to be eating it *with* them. It's the social pressure, the guys offering me a slice and then staring at me when I say no. That’s what I avoid. But just being exposed to other people eating junk food? Or my own schedule getting thrown off by a call? No, I haven't really noticed any anxiety about that kind of stuff. It's really just focused on my own body, not what anyone else is doing.\n\nDoctor: Even though you’ve told me that your uniform and turnout gear are getting loose and the department doctor told you your weight was too low, do you still feel like you need to lose more weight or that you’re not lean enough?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Yeah, I do. It’s... it’s like a constant tape playing in my head, honestly. You'd think hearing the medical officer tell me I'm too light would be this big relief, right? Like, \"Okay, I did it, I can stop now.\" But it doesn't work like that at all. It’s almost the opposite. Because now there's this massive, overwhelming fear of going backwards. \n\nLike, we have this old analog scale in the locker room at the station—you know, the heavy-duty medical kind with the metal sliding weights that clank around when you step on it. I check it pretty much every single shift. And if that scale is up... I mean, even just one single pound... I completely lose it internally. Even if I can logically tell myself, \"Hey, you just drank a ton of extra water because you were doing overhaul on a fire in 90-degree heat,\" or maybe it's just from a big station meal if I actually forced myself to eat a few bites just to get the guys off my back... it doesn't matter. The logic just completely vanishes. \n\nI get this immediate, tight panic in my chest. It’s this horrible physical sensation, like my lungs can't expand, and my brain just starts screaming that I’ve completely blown it. All I can think is that if I gain even an ounce, it's a slippery slope, and suddenly everyone in the department is going to look at me and see me as soft. They're going to think I'm weak, or that I'm just totally out of control of myself and my body. \n\nAnd in my line of work, being out of control is the absolute worst thing you can be. If you're out of control, you're a liability to the guy next to you in a burning building. You have to be disciplined. So, yeah, even with my bunker pants practically falling off me and the doc pulling me into his office to give me warnings, the thought of gaining weight just terrifies me. I just feel like I have to keep pushing, keep leaning out, just to make absolutely sure I never give anyone a reason to look at me and think I've lost my edge.\n\nDoctor: Is your sense of self-worth tied *exclusively* to your body shape or weight, or are there other areas of your life—like your job performance, relationships, or hobbies—that also strongly influence how you see yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I guess it's hard to separate them, honestly. I wouldn't say it's *exclusively* my weight or my shape, because my job performance means absolutely everything to me. Being a firefighter isn't just what I do, it's who I am, you know? I take a massive amount of pride in being good at my job, knowing my equipment, being someone the guys can rely on when things go sideways. So my self-worth is definitely tied to that. \n\nBut the thing is... they’re kind of tangled up together now. I can't really separate how I feel about my body from how I feel about my job, because one completely dictates the other. \n\nLike, every single morning before work, before I even put on my station uniform, I have this... I guess you could call it a routine, though I don't really want to do it. I get up, and I go straight to the bathroom mirror. And I'll just stand there under those harsh overhead lights, turning side to side, and I just intensely focus on whether my waist and my face look sharp enough. I'm looking for that specific definition, checking if my jawline is cut, checking if my stomach is completely flat and tight. I'll stand there pulling my shirt tight against my torso, just analyzing every little angle. \n\nAnd if they don’t look sharp—if I wake up and I feel like my face looks even a tiny bit puffy, or if my waist feels soft to me—it completely ruins everything. It completely messes with how confident and command-ready I feel that day. \n\nBecause in this line of work, you have to project authority, right? When we roll up on a scene, whether it's a medical call or a structure fire, people are usually having the worst day of their lives. They look at us to take control of the chaos. And if I look in the mirror that morning and feel like I look soft, I just... I don't feel like I belong in that uniform. I feel like an imposter. I'll spend the entire 24-hour shift second-guessing myself, adjusting my posture, worrying that the captain or the public is looking at me and thinking I don't have the discipline to be there. So, yeah, my job and being a good firefighter are a huge part of how I see myself, but if my body doesn't look exactly the way I think it needs to in that mirror, it just totally strips away all that confidence before I even walk through the bay doors.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I can't really separate who I am as a person from what the mirror shows me or what the scale says. They're just completely tied together at this point, and I don't even know how to untangle them anymore. It's just all one and the same to me.\n\nDoctor: Has your focus on your body shape or weight caused problems in your relationships, work, or other important areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Well, as far as the actual work goes, no, it hasn't caused problems there. I’m still doing my job, and I’m doing it well. I mean, when the tones drop, I’m the first one on the rig, gear on, ready to work. I’m still pulling my weight on the fireground, maybe even more so because I feel like I have to prove myself constantly. So tactically, functionally, I'm fine. \n\nBut... yeah, I guess it is causing problems in other ways, specifically with the guys. You have to understand, in a firehouse, the kitchen is everything. It’s the absolute center of the universe. That’s where you bond, where you debrief after a rough call, where you bust each other's chops and build that trust. Station meals are a massive tradition. But lately, I’ve just... I’ve started ducking out of station meals completely. \n\nLike, if it's our turn to cook and someone is making a huge baked ziti, or if the crew decides to pool money and order a ton of wings or Chinese food, I just can't be around it. I’ll find any excuse to be somewhere else. I’ll tell the captain I need to go do a deep clean of the compartments on the engine, or I’ll go inventory the EMS bags for the third time that shift, or I’ll just hide out in the bunk room and pretend I'm intensely studying for the lieutenant's exam. I just keep to myself when the crew orders food. \n\nAnd the thing is, it’s really starting to isolate me. I’m definitely less part of the team than I used to be, and that... that really gets to me, because the brotherhood is honestly the best part of this job. At first, the guys just thought I was on some hardcore temporary health kick. They’d make the usual jokes, call me a rabbit, offer me a piece of lettuce, whatever. Just standard firehouse ribbing. But now... now it’s just awkward. They don't even bother calling me over the PA when dinner's ready anymore. I’ll be out in the apparatus bay by myself, wiping down tools, and I can hear them in the day room, laughing and telling stories and just being a crew, and I'm entirely on the outside of it. \n\nIt feels like there's this thick glass wall between me and the rest of my shift now. I’m still working right beside them, trusting them with my life in a fire, but I’m not really *with* them anymore. And I hate it. I want to sit at that table and joke around like I used to, but the thought of sitting there, having to either eat what they're eating or have them stare at me while I drink black coffee... the anxiety is just too loud. So I just keep avoiding them, even though I know it's ruining my relationships at the station.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your body is heavier or less toned than it actually is, even when others might tell you otherwise?\n\nPatient: It’s honestly really confusing because, logically, I know the facts, right? Like, I have the physical evidence right in front of me. My gear and my station clothes... they’re hanging so much looser on me now. I wear these standard-issue navy uniform pants around the firehouse, and lately, I’m having to cinch my belt up a couple of extra notches just to keep them from sliding down when I’m walking around the apparatus bay. And my actual turnout gear, the heavy stuff we wear into a fire? I feel like I’m practically swimming in my bunker pants these days. I had to adjust my suspenders all the way down just so the crotch doesn't hang at my knees. So physically, I know things have changed. \n\nAnd on top of that, we actually just had our annual departmental fitness reviews. It’s this whole mandatory physical and agility test you have to do to prove you’re fit for duty. Well, the medical officer actually flagged my chart this time. He pulled me into his office, shut the door, and gave me this whole serious speech because he said my weight was officially too low for my height and my natural build. He was looking right at the numbers on the scale, telling me I need to be careful, asking if I was feeling weak or lightheaded on calls, making this huge deal out of it and acting like I was on the verge of collapsing on the fireground. \n\nBut... and this is where I know I sound completely irrational... even after he said all that, and even with my clothes literally bagging off me, I walked out of that medical review and immediately thought, well, he doesn't know what he's talking about. Because when I look in the mirror, I just don't see a guy whose weight is too low. I still see softness. I still see areas that aren't defined enough, especially around my waist. So even with the department doctor flagging me and telling me I'm underweight, part of me still feels like I should keep trimming down just a little more. \n\nIt's like I can't trust what anyone else is saying, or even what my own clothes are doing, because my brain is just convinced that I'm still heavier than I need to be to get that perfect, command-ready look I was talking about. I'll stand there pulling at my shirt, thinking that if I just lean out a tiny bit more, I'll finally look the way I'm supposed to. I just keep thinking, just a little more, just a little more, and it doesn't matter what the fitness review says, because I just don't see what they see.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience significant distress or anxiety when you are unable to follow your dietary routine or when you are exposed to situations where others are eating foods you consider unhealthy?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I wouldn't really say I get distressed about a routine, because, honestly, I don't really have one of those strict routines to begin with. Like, you know those guys at the station, the total gym rats? They’ll bring in their stack of little meal-prep Tupperware containers with exactly six ounces of boiled chicken breast and half a cup of plain brown rice, and if the tones drop for a call and they end up missing their perfectly scheduled 12:00 PM feeding, they completely lose their minds. They get all cranky and start complaining about losing their gains or whatever. \n\nI’m not like that at all. I don't have this rigid schedule where I have to eat a certain thing at a certain time, so there isn't really a routine for me to mess up or get anxious over. Mostly, I'm just... you know, skipping it, or just grabbing a black coffee, or maybe something really small if I absolutely have to so I don't feel shaky. So if a call goes long—like last week, we had this massive three-alarm commercial building fire that kept us out on the scene for like eight hours straight—I don't panic about missing a meal. If anything, it's just easier. I don't have to think about it.\n\nAnd as for other people eating stuff... nah, that doesn't bother me at all. I mean, I know I just said a minute ago that I avoid the big station dinners, but that's not because seeing the food itself makes me anxious, or because I'm judging the guys for what they're eating. I really don't care if the crew is housing a dozen glazed donuts from the bakery down the street or ordering deep-dish pizza. They can eat whatever they want, they work hard, they burn it off. I don't look at them and feel distressed or think, \"Wow, that's so unhealthy.\" \n\nIf I'm just walking through the day room and Smitty is sitting in the recliner eating a massive meatball sub, I don't feel any anxiety about *him* eating it. I’ll still stop and talk to him for a second about the baseball game last night. The problem for me is just the expectation that I'm supposed to be eating it *with* them. It's the social pressure, the guys offering me a slice and then staring at me when I say no. That’s what I avoid. But just being exposed to other people eating junk food? Or my own schedule getting thrown off by a call? No, I haven't really noticed any anxiety about that kind of stuff. It's really just focused on my own body, not what anyone else is doing.\n\nDoctor: Even though you’ve told me that your uniform and turnout gear are getting loose and the department doctor told you your weight was too low, do you still feel like you need to lose more weight or that you’re not lean enough?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Yeah, I do. It’s... it’s like a constant tape playing in my head, honestly. You'd think hearing the medical officer tell me I'm too light would be this big relief, right? Like, \"Okay, I did it, I can stop now.\" But it doesn't work like that at all. It’s almost the opposite. Because now there's this massive, overwhelming fear of going backwards. \n\nLike, we have this old analog scale in the locker room at the station—you know, the heavy-duty medical kind with the metal sliding weights that clank around when you step on it. I check it pretty much every single shift. And if that scale is up... I mean, even just one single pound... I completely lose it internally. Even if I can logically tell myself, \"Hey, you just drank a ton of extra water because you were doing overhaul on a fire in 90-degree heat,\" or maybe it's just from a big station meal if I actually forced myself to eat a few bites just to get the guys off my back... it doesn't matter. The logic just completely vanishes. \n\nI get this immediate, tight panic in my chest. It’s this horrible physical sensation, like my lungs can't expand, and my brain just starts screaming that I’ve completely blown it. All I can think is that if I gain even an ounce, it's a slippery slope, and suddenly everyone in the department is going to look at me and see me as soft. They're going to think I'm weak, or that I'm just totally out of control of myself and my body. \n\nAnd in my line of work, being out of control is the absolute worst thing you can be. If you're out of control, you're a liability to the guy next to you in a burning building. You have to be disciplined. So, yeah, even with my bunker pants practically falling off me and the doc pulling me into his office to give me warnings, the thought of gaining weight just terrifies me. I just feel like I have to keep pushing, keep leaning out, just to make absolutely sure I never give anyone a reason to look at me and think I've lost my edge.\n\nDoctor: Is your sense of self-worth tied *exclusively* to your body shape or weight, or are there other areas of your life—like your job performance, relationships, or hobbies—that also strongly influence how you see yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I guess it's hard to separate them, honestly. I wouldn't say it's *exclusively* my weight or my shape, because my job performance means absolutely everything to me. Being a firefighter isn't just what I do, it's who I am, you know? I take a massive amount of pride in being good at my job, knowing my equipment, being someone the guys can rely on when things go sideways. So my self-worth is definitely tied to that. \n\nBut the thing is... they’re kind of tangled up together now. I can't really separate how I feel about my body from how I feel about my job, because one completely dictates the other. \n\nLike, every single morning before work, before I even put on my station uniform, I have this... I guess you could call it a routine, though I don't really want to do it. I get up, and I go straight to the bathroom mirror. And I'll just stand there under those harsh overhead lights, turning side to side, and I just intensely focus on whether my waist and my face look sharp enough. I'm looking for that specific definition, checking if my jawline is cut, checking if my stomach is completely flat and tight. I'll stand there pulling my shirt tight against my torso, just analyzing every little angle. \n\nAnd if they don’t look sharp—if I wake up and I feel like my face looks even a tiny bit puffy, or if my waist feels soft to me—it completely ruins everything. It completely messes with how confident and command-ready I feel that day. \n\nBecause in this line of work, you have to project authority, right? When we roll up on a scene, whether it's a medical call or a structure fire, people are usually having the worst day of their lives. They look at us to take control of the chaos. And if I look in the mirror that morning and feel like I look soft, I just... I don't feel like I belong in that uniform. I feel like an imposter. I'll spend the entire 24-hour shift second-guessing myself, adjusting my posture, worrying that the captain or the public is looking at me and thinking I don't have the discipline to be there. So, yeah, my job and being a good firefighter are a huge part of how I see myself, but if my body doesn't look exactly the way I think it needs to in that mirror, it just totally strips away all that confidence before I even walk through the bay doors."
  }
